```text
1	\section{Problem Setup and Methods}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Safety Commitment Point}
5	\label{sec:commitment-point}
6	
7	When a reasoning model is jailbroken, its chain-of-thought (CoT) transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation. We define the \textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP) as the first reasoning step after which safety reasoning permanently ceases. Steps before CP are \emph{pre-commitment} ($y_t = 0$); steps at or after CP are \emph{post-commitment} ($y_t = 1$). The detection task produces a score $s_t \in [0, 1]$ estimating whether the model has passed its CP by step $t$.
8	
9	We study four reasoning models spanning two architectures and two scales (\cref{tab:models}).
10	
11	\begin{table}[t]
12	\centering
13	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
14	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
15	\toprule
16	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Arch.} & \textbf{Params} & \textbf{Layers} & \textbf{Hidden} & \textbf{$n$ (total\,/\,test)} \\
17	\midrule
18	R1-8B & Llama & 8B & 32 & 4096 & 209\,/\,43 \\
19	OT-7B & Qwen & 7B & 28 & 3584 & 228\,/\,47 \\
20	R1-32B & Qwen & 32B & 64 & 5120 & 100\,/\,20 \\
21	QwQ-32B & Qwen & 32B & 64 & 5120 & 95\,/\,19 \\
22	\bottomrule
23	\end{tabular}}
24	\caption{Models studied. R1-8B and R1-32B are distilled from DeepSeek-R1~\citep{deepseekr1}; QwQ-32B~\citep{qwq} is a native reasoning model; OT-7B~\citep{openthinker} is trained with STILL~\citep{still} on Qwen2.5-7B. Data: HarmThoughts~\citep{harmthoughts} with step-level CP annotations. Split: 60/20/20.}
25	\label{tab:models}
26	\end{table}
27	
28	\subsection{Probe Architecture}
29	\label{sec:probe-architecture}
30	
31	\paragraph{Hidden-state (HS) probes.} At each step $t$, we extract the last-token hidden state $\mathbf{h}_t^{(\ell)} \in \mathbb{R}^d$ from layer $\ell$ and train an L2-regularized logistic regression classifier (balanced class weights, $C{=}1.0$, LBFGS, max\_iter${}=2000$) on binary CP labels. Layer selection is analyzed in \cref{sec:layer-sensitivity}.
32	
33	\paragraph{Text probes.} Each step's text is encoded with all-MiniLM-L6-v2~\citep{wang2020minilm} ($\mathbb{R}^{384}$), a distilled sentence encoder from the Sentence-BERT family~\citep{reimers2019sentence}. The same logistic regression is applied as a surface-level modality baseline.
34	
35	\paragraph{Temporal windowing.} For window size $W$, we concatenate features from steps $[t{-}W{+}1, \ldots, t]$. We evaluate $W \in \{1, 3, 5, 10, 15, 20, 25\}$ and select per-model on validation. GRU sequence models are also evaluated under two protocols: a 70/30 train/test split (primary) and the same 60/20/20 split used for LR experiments (for fair comparison; \cref{tab:gru_multi_seeds,app:gru_details}).
36	
37	\subsection{Layer Selection Sensitivity}
38	\label{sec:layer-sensitivity}
39	
40	Layer selection is a researcher degree of freedom that can reverse modality comparison conclusions. A composite metric (balanced accuracy $\times$ crossing rate) systematically selects shallow layers for 32B models: L2 (3\% depth) for QwQ-32B, L10 (16\%) for R1-32B. Shallow representations are highly compressed (inter-step cosine distances 1.4--2.7$\times$ smaller than at deep layers), so a linear probe operates near the noise floor and threshold crossings are noise-driven (\cref{app:cosine_volatility}).
41	
42	A five-metric ablation reveals that threshold-dependent metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) consistently select deep layers: L60 (94\%) for QwQ-32B, L63 (98\%) for R1-32B. Pure balanced accuracy selects layers whose depth varies across data splits and models (7--98\% under 60/20/20; \cref{tab:ba_layer_comparison}). At these layers, all models$^\dagger$ show significant HS advantage with evidence from strong to qualified (Holm-Bonferroni corrected, \cref{sec:evaluation}); at biased layers, only the two 7--8B models survive. While we report five metrics, their information content is not independent: BA, precision, and FPR share a confusion matrix with 2 degrees of freedom (given class marginals); crossing rate and the additive composite are derived from these. The effective number of independent statistical tests is four (one per model), as reflected in our HB correction. The five-metric ablation diagnoses which \emph{aspect} of classification performance drives layer selection bias, not five independent pieces of evidence. Holm-Bonferroni step-down on four model-level tests yields the same result (all pass, with R1-8B FPR $p{=}0.044$ at rank-4 threshold $\alpha/1{=}0.050$). A joint (layer, $W$) grid search over 192 configurations confirms that L60 is near-optimal for QwQ-32B at recommended windows ($W{=}3$--$5$; \cref{app:layer_sweep}). Full per-layer results appear in the same appendix.
43	
44	\subsection{Baseline Methods}
45	\label{sec:baselines}
46	
47	We evaluate seven alternatives to contextualize probe performance (\cref{tab:negative}): BOCPD~\citep{adams2007bayesian} and CUSUM~\citep{page1954cusum} for change-point detection; Mahalanobis distance following TrajGuard~\citep{trajguard}; RepE mean-difference directions~\citep{zou2023representation}; two-layer MLP probes; CCS-adapted~\citep{burns2023discovering}; and cross-model transfer via PCA. A position-only baseline ($t/T$) controls for temporal artifacts. Detailed configurations in \cref{app:bocpd_config,app:transfer_details}.
48	
49	\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}
50	\label{sec:evaluation}
51	
52	We report balanced accuracy, precision, pre-CP FPR, coverage, and lead time (steps between first detection and true CP). Step-level metrics evaluate per-step classification; trace-level metrics aggregate across an entire reasoning trace (e.g., trace-level FPR is the fraction of safe traces with any false alarm exceeding $K$ consecutive crossings). Detection requires $K{=}5$ consecutive threshold crossings; $K$ was set as a design pre-selection before examining performance; sensitivity analysis (\cref{app:k_ablation}) confirms that $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$ produces smooth coverage-FPR tradeoffs without qualitative changes in the modality comparison, though a post-hoc rationale for $K{=}5$ is that it corresponds to a sustained commitment signal rather than transient fluctuation. Modality comparisons use two-sided null-centered bootstrap (10{,}000 resamples) with Holm-Bonferroni step-down correction (3 metrics $\times$ 4 models $= 12$ tests, $\alpha{=}0.05$). Within each model, the three metrics are positively correlated by construction, making the correction stricter than under independence. Control task selectivity~\citep{hewitt2019control} validates genuine representational structure (\cref{app:control}). Throughout, $\dagger$ marks conclusions drawn from sample-limited test sets ($n \leq 20$): R1-32B ($n{=}20$) and QwQ-32B ($n{=}19$).
53	
```

_source line 523 · 2026-05-18T17:04:26.694Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01TkdupRduZEbkAazLoUXavP`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Experiments}\label{sec:experiments}
2	
3	We evaluate temporal probes across four reasoning models (\cref{tab:models}), comparing hidden-state (HS) and text modalities under different layer selection criteria.
4	
5	\paragraph{Evidence gradient.} The strength of HS advantage evidence varies by model, not scale. We classify evidence into three tiers based on seed robustness, position independence, and sample size: \textbf{strong} (OT-7B: 5/5 seeds HB, $\Delta$Prec $+20.9{\pm}1.3$pp, no position confound, selectivity 18.3pp, $n{=}47$); \textbf{moderate} (R1-8B: precision-robust but FPR seed-fragile at 3/5 HB, $n{=}43$; QwQ-32B$^\dagger$: 5/5 HB, $+19.9{\pm}2.5$pp, negative lead time, $n{=}19$); and \textbf{qualified} (R1-32B$^\dagger$: 5/5 HB, $\Delta$Prec std${=}2.0$pp, but position-mediated with residualized gap NS, $W{=}1$ only, $n{=}20$).
6	
7	\subsection{Setup}\label{sec:setup}
8	
9	We study R1-8B (Llama-8B, R1-distilled, $n{=}43$ test traces), OT-7B (OpenThinker-7B, trained on the OpenThoughts dataset~\citep{openthinker}; Qwen-7B backbone, STILL-trained, $n{=}47$), QwQ-32B (Qwen-32B, native reasoning, $n{=}19$--$20$), and R1-32B (Qwen-32B, R1-distilled, $n{=}20$). All traces come from HarmThoughts with step-level CP annotations (60/20/20 split). HS probes extract last-token hidden states from a selected layer; text probes encode each step with all-MiniLM-L6-v2 (384d). Both use L2-regularized logistic regression with temporal windowing $W \in \{1, 3, 5, 10, 15, 20, 25\}$ selected on validation. Detection requires $K{=}5$ consecutive threshold crossings. Statistical testing follows \cref{sec:evaluation}.
10	
11	\subsection{Layer Selection Results}\label{sec:layer-results}
12	
13	\cref{tab:layer_selection} shows that the biased composite metric (balanced accuracy $\times$ crossing rate) systematically selects shallow layers for 32B models: L2 (3\% depth) for QwQ-32B and L10 (16\%) for R1-32B. Three of four metrics excluding crossing rate (precision, inverse FPR, and additive composite) consistently select deep layers: L60 (94\%) and L63 (98\%). Pure balanced accuracy, a threshold-free metric, selects layers that vary across data splits and models (7--98\% depth under 60/20/20; \cref{tab:ba_layer_comparison}), itself demonstrating that layer selection is a consequential methodological choice. The mechanism is that shallow layers produce highly compressed representations with small inter-step margins: step-to-step cosine distances are 1.4--2.7$\times$ smaller than at deep layers across all models (\cref{app:cosine_volatility}). A linear probe in this compressed space operates near the noise floor, generating frequent threshold crossings that inflate crossing rate independently of discriminative power (\cref{sec:layer-sensitivity}).
14	
15	\begin{table}[t]
16	\centering
17	\small
18	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
19	\toprule
20	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Biased}$^a$ & \textbf{Corrected}$^b$ & $p$ \textbf{(corr.)} \\
21	\midrule
22	R1-8B & L14 (44\%) & L14 (44\%) & ${<}0.001$ \\
23	OT-7B & L0 (0\%) & L16 (57\%) & ${<}0.001$ \\
24	QwQ-32B & L2 (3\%) & L60 (94\%) & ${<}0.005$ \\
25	R1-32B & L10 (16\%) & L63 (98\%) & ${<}0.001$ \\
26	\bottomrule
27	\end{tabular}
28	\caption{Layer selection sensitivity. $^a$\,bal\_acc $\times$ crossing\_rate. $^b$\,Consensus of threshold-dependent metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite). Rightmost: HS vs.\ text precision $p$-value at corrected layer (e.g., QwQ-32B biased-layer: bal\_acc $p{=}0.35$, precision $p{=}0.83$, both non-significant).}
29	\label{tab:layer_selection}
30	\end{table}
31	
32	\begin{figure}[t]
33	\centering
34	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_layer_heatmap.pdf}
35	\caption{Per-layer metric heatmap (2$\times$2). Each column: one of five metrics (min-max normalized). Red dashed: biased layer (bal\_acc $\times$ crossing\_rate); green dashed: corrected layer (consensus of threshold-dependent metrics). For R1-8B, both coincide at L14. At 32B, biased selections cluster near input layers while threshold-dependent metrics peak at deep layers.}
36	\label{fig:layer_heatmap}
37	\end{figure}
38	
39	The downstream impact is severe: at biased layers, only the two 7--8B models show significant HS advantage; both 32B models fail. At corrected layers, all models pass Holm-Bonferroni correction$^\dagger$ (\cref{sec:evaluation}), from strong (OT-7B) to qualified (R1-32B). Had we reported only biased-layer results, we would have concluded a scale-dependent HS advantage that is entirely a methodological artifact.
40	
41	\subsection{HS vs.\ Text Comparison}\label{sec:hs-text}
42	
43	\begin{table}[t]
44	\centering
45	\small
46	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
47	\begin{tabular}{llclrrlrc}
48	\toprule
49	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Layer} & \textbf{Enc.} & $W$ & $\Delta$\textbf{BA} & $\Delta$\textbf{Prec} & \textbf{95\% CI} & $\Delta$\textbf{FPR} & \textbf{HB} \\
50	\midrule
51	R1-8B & L14 & 384d & 15 & +8.2 & \textbf{+32.5} & [+20.2, +45.2] & $-$7.4 & \checkmark \\
52	 & & 1024d & & \multicolumn{1}{c}{--} & +5.8 & [$-$7.4, +19.5] & \multicolumn{1}{c}{--} & \\[2pt]
53	OT-7B & L16 & 384d & 3 & +10.7 & +21.9 & [+14.4, +29.6] & $-$17.1 & \checkmark \\
54	 & & 1024d & & \multicolumn{1}{c}{--} & +18.1 & [+8.4, +29.0] & \multicolumn{1}{c}{--} & \checkmark \\[2pt]
55	QwQ & L60 & 384d & 3 & +15.4 & +23.6 & [+12.9, +35.1] & $-$9.8 & \checkmark \\
56	 & & 1024d & & \multicolumn{1}{c}{--} & +9.4 & [$-$7.1, +27.7] & \multicolumn{1}{c}{--} & \\[2pt]
57	R1-32B & L63 & 384d & 1 & \textbf{+18.7} & +15.4 & [+8.7, +22.6] & \textbf{$-$14.6} & \checkmark \\
58	 & & 1024d & & \multicolumn{1}{c}{--} & +11.7 & [$-$0.2, +24.3] & \multicolumn{1}{c}{--} & \\
59	\bottomrule
60	\end{tabular}}
61	\caption{HS vs.\ text at corrected layers (LR probes, 60/20/20 split). Enc.: text encoder (384d = MiniLM-L6-v2, 1024d = BGE-large). $\Delta$: HS$-$text difference (pp); ``--'' = metric not computed for that encoder comparison. 95\% CI: bootstrap interval for $\Delta$Prec (10{,}000 resamples). HB: \checkmark{} = model-level test passes Holm-Bonferroni ($\alpha{=}0.05$); all 384d model-level tests pass$^\dagger$. 1024d: HB over 4 model-level precision tests. Values from canonical split; 5-seed mean$\pm$std in \cref{app:multi_seed}. R1-32B: precision std${=}0.128$, FPR std${=}0.145$ across 5 seeds (\cref{app:multi_seed}). BGE $\Delta$BA/$\Delta$FPR in \cref{app:encoder_robustness}.}
62	\label{tab:hs_vs_text}
63	\end{table}
64	
65	Against the 384d baseline, HS probes significantly outperform text probes across all models$^\dagger$ on all three metrics (\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}), though R1-8B FPR significance is fragile across seeds (2/5 uncorrected, 1/5 Bonferroni-corrected; \cref{tab:r18b_fpr_seeds}). At 7--8B, the evidence is strong: precision gains of +32.5pp (R1-8B) and +21.9pp (OT-7B), stable across seeds (std ${<}4$pp). R1-8B FPR advantage is directionally consistent across seeds (5/5) but not robust to data split (2/5 reach $p{<}0.05$ uncorrected; after Bonferroni correction for 5 seeds, 1/5 remains significant; \cref{app:multi_seed}). At 32B, both models are significant (HB corrected) but with model-specific caveats: R1-32B achieves significance only at $W{=}1$ with 25\% coverage (5/20 traces), and QwQ-32B's position baseline is competitive (\cref{sec:32b-analysis}). R1-32B passes 5/5 seeds (paired $\Delta$Prec std${=}2.0$pp, despite absolute precision std${=}12.8$pp) but its advantage is position-mediated (residualized gap NS; \cref{app:position_residual}). Overall, 18/20 model-seed combinations pass full HB; R1-8B FPR instability accounts for both failures (\cref{app:multi_seed}). Control task selectivity confirms genuine safety signal: 12.5--19.7pp above chance (\cref{app:control}).
66	
67	\paragraph{Capacity control.} PCA reduction of HS features to 384d (matching text encoder dimensionality, 79--92\% variance retained) preserves the precision advantage across all models$^\dagger$ (+16.8 to +23.1pp, all $p{<}0.012$), with all model-level precision tests passing Holm-Bonferroni. Two individual metric comparisons are non-significant: R1-8B balanced accuracy (+1.3pp, $p{=}0.731$, effect too small at $n{=}43$) and QwQ-32B FPR ($-$11.4pp, $p{=}0.038$, below HB threshold). PCA retains directions of maximum variance in hidden-state space, which need not align with the semantic dimensions captured by a trained text encoder; the control provides partial evidence against a pure capacity explanation but does not rule out representation quality differences between PCA-compressed HS and trained text encoders.
68	
69	\paragraph{Encoder robustness.} The 1024d rows in \cref{tab:hs_vs_text} replace MiniLM (384d) with BGE-large~\citep{xiao2023cpack} (1024d, a 2.7$\times$ stronger encoder). The HS precision advantage persists directionally across all models$^\dagger$ but narrows substantially: only OT-7B survives Holm-Bonferroni (1/4 precision tests). HS balanced accuracy advantage is also directionally positive across all models$^\dagger$ (+8.4 to +10.9pp), with 2/4 (OT-7B, R1-32B) significant under Holm-Bonferroni; FPR advantage is significant only for OT-7B (1/4). R1-8B shows an adverse FPR direction (HS FPR 1.2pp higher than BGE, $p{=}0.564$). The advantage is encoder-conditional rather than absolute (\cref{fig:forest_precision}). Layer selection (Contribution 1) is encoder-independent, as it concerns hidden-state properties across layers rather than the text baseline.
70	
71	\paragraph{Position mediation.} Position ($t/T$) accounts for 9--20pp of balanced accuracy depending on model and window size,\footnote{R1-32B's 20.4pp drop reflects $W{=}1$ (no temporal context); at $W{\geq}3$ with temporal windowing, drops are 9--17pp.} but all models retain non-positional signal above chance (all $p{<}0.001$; \cref{app:position_residual}). Three of four models retain significant non-positional HS advantage over text (residualized gap +3.7 to +6.9pp, HB corrected); R1-32B's gap is +1.9pp ($p{=}0.914$, $n{=}20$), consistent with a position-mediated advantage. Temporal windowing reduces position dependence (R1-8B: 24.3pp drop at $W{=}3$ vs.\ 16.5pp at $W{=}15$).
72	
73	\paragraph{Caveats.} R1-32B achieves significance only at $W{=}1$ with 25\% coverage (5/20 traces); at $W{\geq}3$, dimensionality ($51200 \times W$ for $n{=}20$) prevents significance. Sweeping regularization $C$ over $[0.01, 100]$ yields ${<}2$pp variation in balanced accuracy; the HS advantage is also stable across decision thresholds (BA variation ${<}1.5$pp across $\tau \in [0.3, 0.7]$; \cref{app:threshold_sensitivity}). QwQ-32B HS lead time is negative ($-$1.2 to +1.9 steps vs.\ text 3.7--12.3 steps): higher precision at later detection. On benign traces, probes produce 39--52\% false alarm rates, so current methods characterize the commitment phenomenon rather than constitute production-ready detectors. Per-model details in \cref{sec:32b-analysis}.
74	
75	\subsection{32B Model-Specific Analysis}\label{sec:32b-analysis}
76	
77	R1-32B and QwQ-32B share the Qwen-32B architecture (64 layers, 5120 hidden) but present opposite detection profiles (\cref{tab:32b_divergence}); training method is the likely differentiator.
78	
79	\begin{table}[t]
80	\centering
81	\small
82	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
83	\toprule
84	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Cov.} & \textbf{Lead (steps)} & \textbf{HB} & \textbf{Pos.\ margin} \\
85	\midrule
86	R1-32B & 25\% & +11.7 to +13.3 & \checkmark{} ($W{=}1$) & +14.2pp \\
87	QwQ-32B & 74\% & $-$1.2 to +1.9 & \checkmark{} (all $W$) & $-$3.6pp \\
88	\bottomrule
89	\end{tabular}
90	\caption{32B cross-model divergence. R1-32B provides limited early detection with narrow coverage; QwQ-32B provides robust significance with poor lead time. HB: \checkmark{} = model-level test passes Holm-Bonferroni ($\alpha{=}0.05$). Pos.\ margin: HS balanced accuracy minus position-only baseline.}
91	\label{tab:32b_divergence}
92	\end{table}
93	
94	R1-32B detects commitment 11--13 steps early with a +14.2pp margin over the position baseline, which confirms genuine representational signal, but covers only 25\% of traces at the single significant window ($W{=}1$). QwQ-32B achieves statistical significance at all window sizes with 74\% coverage, but HS probes fire later than text probes (negative lead time). Consistent with the universal position analysis (\cref{sec:hs-text}), QwQ-32B's position baseline achieves higher balanced accuracy (0.832 vs.\ 0.796). HS precision (+4.5pp, $p{=}0.133$) and FPR (+2.5pp, $p{=}0.279$) margins over the position baseline are directionally positive but not statistically significant at $n{=}19$. The divergence implies that per-model validation is essential: no single detection configuration generalizes even within the same architecture at the same scale, and a system validated on one 32B model would fail on the other.
95	
96	\subsection{Temporal Context}\label{sec:temporal}
97	
98	Temporal windowing ($W{>}1$) improves both modalities on all models (\cref{fig:window_ablation}). Text precision improves +8 to +23pp from $W{=}1$ to $W{=}25$; HS precision improves +1.7 to +12.9pp. At 7--8B, the modality advantage (+22 to +33pp precision) exceeds the temporal improvement (+13pp). Both effects are complementary: temporal windowing ($W{=}3$--$15$) combined with principled layer selection yields the most consistent gains. Full per-model temporal ablations appear in \cref{app:temporal_details}; GRU sequence models under a matched 60/20/20 split achieve comparable accuracy but higher FPR than LR probes (\cref{tab:gru_multi_seeds}).
99	
100	\begin{figure}[t]
101	\centering
102	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_forest_precision.pdf}
103	\caption{Forest plot of HS precision advantage over text probes. Blue: vs.\ MiniLM-384d; orange: vs.\ BGE-1024d. Solid markers: survives Holm-Bonferroni; hollow: does not. All 384d comparisons are significant (strong to qualified evidence); against BGE-1024d, only OT-7B (strong) survives correction.}
104	\label{fig:forest_precision}
105	\end{figure}
106	
107	\subsection{Negative Results}\label{sec:negative}
108	
109	No alternative method consistently matches supervised probing across models$^\dagger$ at any evidence tier (\cref{tab:negative}). Each failure is diagnostic: BOCPD~\citep{adams2007bayesian}/CUSUM fail on gradual transitions; Mahalanobis distance finds near-zero separation (signal in minor PCA components, $<$5\% variance); RepE~\citep{zou2023representation} is model-dependent (QwQ-32B BA 0.756 but precision 0.512 vs.\ R1-32B BA 0.559); MLP probes overfit (3/4 worse than LR); CCS misaligns with temporal structure; cross-model transfer fails across 17 configurations. Detailed analysis and the full comparison table in \cref{app:negative_details}.
110

Three of four models retain significant non-positional HS advantage over text (residualized gap +3.7 to +6.9pp, HB corrected); R1-32B's gap is +1.9pp ($p{=}0.914$, $n{=}20$), consistent with a position-mediated advantage. Temporal windowing reduces position dependence (R1-8B: 24.3pp drop at $W{=}3$ vs.\\ 16.5pp at $W{=}15$).\n\n\\paragraph{Caveats.} R1-32B achieves significance only at $W{=}1$ with 25\\% coverage (5/20 traces); at $W{\\geq}3$, dimensionality ($51200 \\times W$ for $n{=}20$) prevents significance. Sweeping regularization $C$ over $[0.01, 100]$ yields ${<}2$pp variation in balanced accuracy; the HS advantage is also stable across decision thresholds (BA variation ${<}1.5$pp across $\\tau \\in [0.3, 0.7]$; \\cref{app:threshold_sensitivity}). QwQ-32B HS lead time is negative ($-$1.2 to +1.9 steps vs.\\ text 3.7--12.3 steps): higher precision at later detection. On benign traces, probes produce 39--52\\% false alarm rates, so current methods characterize the commitment phenomenon rather than constitute production-ready detectors. Per-model details in \\cref{sec:32b-analysis}.\n\n\\subsection{32B Model-Specific Analysis}\\label{sec:32b-analysis}\n\nR1-32B and QwQ-32B share the Qwen-32B architecture (64 layers, 5120 hidden) but present opposite detection profiles (\\cref{tab:32b_divergence}); training method is the likely differentiator.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Cov.} & \\textbf{Lead (steps)} & \\textbf{HB} & \\textbf{Pos.\\ margin} \\\\\n\\midrule\nR1-32B & 25\\% & +11.7 to +13.3 & \\checkmark{} ($W{=}1$) & +14.2pp \\\\\nQwQ-32B & 74\\% & $-$1.2 to +1.9 & \\checkmark{} (all $W$) & $-$3.6pp \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{32B cross-model divergence. R1-32B provides limited early detection with narrow coverage; QwQ-32B provides robust significance with poor lead time. HB: \\checkmark{} = model-level test passes Holm-Bonferroni ($\\alpha{=}0.05$). Pos.\\ margin: HS balanced accuracy minus position-only baseline.}\n\\label{tab:32b_divergence}\n\\end{table}\n\nR1-32B detects commitment 11--13 steps early with a +14.2pp margin over the position baseline, which confirms genuine representational signal, but covers only 25\\% of traces at the single significant window ($W{=}1$). QwQ-32B achieves statistical significance at all window sizes with 74\\% coverage, but HS probes fire later than text probes (negative lead time). Consistent with the universal position analysis (\\cref{sec:hs-text}), QwQ-32B's position baseline achieves higher balanced accuracy (0.832 vs.\\ 0.796). HS precision (+4.5pp, $p{=}0.133$) and FPR (+2.5pp, $p{=}0.279$) margins over the position baseline are directionally positive but not statistically significant at $n{=}19$. The divergence implies that per-model validation is essential: no single detection configuration generalizes even within the same architecture at the same scale, and a system validated on one 32B model would fail on the other.\n\n\\subsection{Temporal Context}\\label{sec:temporal}\n\nTemporal windowing ($W{>}1$) improves both modalities on all models (\\cref{fig:window_ablation}). Text precision improves +8 to +23pp from $W{=}1$ to $W{=}25$; HS precision improves +1.7 to +12.9pp. At 7--8B, the modality advantage (+22 to +33pp precision) exceeds the temporal improvement (+13pp). Both effects are complementary: temporal windowing ($W{=}3$--$15$) combined with principled layer selection yields the most consistent gains. Full per-model temporal ablations appear in \\cref{app:temporal_details}; GRU sequence models under a matched 60/20/20 split achieve comparable accuracy but higher FPR than LR probes (\\cref{tab:gru_multi_seeds}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_forest_precision.pdf}\n\\caption{Forest plot of HS precision advantage over text probes. Blue: vs.\\ MiniLM-384d; orange: vs.\\ BGE-1024d. Solid markers: survives Holm-Bonferroni; hollow: does not. All 384d comparisons are significant (strong to qualified evidence); against BGE-1024d, only OT-7B (strong) survives correction.}\n\\label{fig:forest_precision}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Negative Results}\\label{sec:negative}\n\nNo alternative method consistently matches supervised probing across models$^\\dagger$ at any evidence tier (\\cref{tab:negative}). Each failure is diagnostic: BOCPD~\\citep{adams2007bayesian}/CUSUM fail on gradual transitions; Mahalanobis distance finds near-zero separation (signal in minor PCA components, $<$5\\% variance); RepE~\\citep{zou2023representation} is model-dependent (QwQ-32B BA 0.756 but precision 0.512 vs.\\ R1-32B BA 0.559); MLP probes overfit (3/4 worse than LR); CCS misaligns with temporal structure; cross-model transfer fails across 17 configurations. Detailed analysis and the full comparison table in \\cref{app:negative_details}.\n",
      "numLines": 110,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 110
    }

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01DeN5V7xuB97jDPTiY1khVv`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom.}
4	Our five-metric ablation (\cref{sec:layer-results}) shows that crossing rate reverses the central finding: at biased layers, the HS advantage appears scale-dependent; at corrected layers, it is significant across all models$^\dagger$ from strong to qualified (\cref{sec:evaluation}). The mechanism is representation compression at shallow layers (\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), which inflates crossing rate independently of discriminative power. The effect likely generalizes to any threshold-based detection study. The HS advantage holds at both BA-optimal and precision-optimal layers regardless of depth: BA-optimal layers span 7--98\% depth across models and splits, yet 14/16 per-model tests pass HB (precision +12.8 to +18.6pp; \cref{tab:ba_layer_comparison}); the two FPR failures come from underpowered 32B test sets ($n{\leq}20$). That BA selection itself produces such depth variation reinforces the methodological finding. For R1-32B, layers L55--L63 at $W{=}1$ form a robust band where all 3/3 metrics pass HB (\cref{app:layer_sweep}).
5	
6	\paragraph{Encoder-quality boundary.}
7	The HS advantage is a function of text encoder quality: significant against 384d MiniLM across all evidence tiers (4/4 models$^\dagger$ HB) but attenuated against 1024d BGE-large (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (+16.8 to +23.1pp), providing partial evidence against a pure capacity explanation. This boundary is itself a finding: the HS advantage represents information not yet captured by current sentence encoders rather than a fundamental modality difference. Temporal context and modality choice are complementary: at 7--8B, the modality advantage exceeds temporal improvement; temporal windowing ($W{=}3$ to $15$) provides consistent gains regardless of modality.
8	
9	\paragraph{Guidance for future research.}
10	The evidence gradient (strong for OT-7B, moderate for R1-8B and QwQ-32B, qualified for R1-32B) indicates that distillation and native reasoning produce qualitatively different safety representation geometry. PCA capacity control, control task selectivity, and position residualization (3/4 models retain significant non-positional HS advantage; \cref{app:position_residual}) provide converging evidence of genuine safety representations, but our approach is correlational. Causal validation via DAS~\citep{geiger2025causal}, LEACE~\citep{belrose2023leace}, or ITI~\citep{li2024iti} is needed to confirm that probed directions are causally active. We recommend a five-step audit for probing studies: (1)~train probes across all layers; (2)~compute $\geq$3 metrics (threshold-free and threshold-dependent); (3)~check for metric disagreement; (4)~report 2nd/3rd best layers; (5)~compute cosine volatility (\cref{app:cosine_volatility}). Steps 3--5 cost negligible compute.
11	
```

665	\label{app:multi_seed}
666	
667	\cref{tab:multi_seed} reports LR probe performance across 5 random seeds at each model's corrected layer and optimal $W$.
668	
669	\begin{table}[h]
670	\centering
671	\small
672	\caption{LR probe stability across 5 random seeds (corrected layers, optimal $W$). 7--8B models are stable; R1-32B shows high variance due to small sample ($n{=}20$).}
673	\label{tab:multi_seed}
674	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
675	\toprule
676	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Precision} & \textbf{Bal.\ Acc} & \textbf{FPR} \\
677	\midrule
678	R1-8B & $0.590 \pm 0.072$ & $0.756 \pm 0.028$ & $0.154 \pm 0.026$ \\
679	OT-7B & $0.421 \pm 0.034$ & $0.633 \pm 0.023$ & $0.157 \pm 0.021$ \\
680	QwQ-32B & $0.651 \pm 0.099$ & $0.731 \pm 0.028$ & $0.133 \pm 0.032$ \\
681	R1-32B & $0.594 \pm 0.128$ & $0.692 \pm 0.056$ & $0.207 \pm 0.145$ \\
682	\bottomrule
683	\end{tabular}
684	\end{table}
685	
686	R1-32B's high FPR variance (std $= 0.145$) reflects a single outlier seed (FPR $= 0.48$), amplified by the small test set ($n{=}20$). Note that R1-32B's absolute precision std (12.8pp) differs markedly from its paired $\Delta$Prec std (2.0pp): HS and text precision covary across seeds.
687	
688	\paragraph{5-seed Holm-Bonferroni pass rates.} \cref{tab:seed_hb} reports HB pass rates per model across 5 seeds. Overall, 18/20 (90\%) model-seed combinations pass full HB (3/3 metrics).
689	
690	\begin{table}[h]
691	\centering
692	\small
693	\caption{5-seed HB pass rates (3/3 metrics per seed). R1-8B failures are FPR-specific.}
694	\label{tab:seed_hb}
695	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
696	\toprule
697	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{HB Pass} & \textbf{Mean $\Delta$Prec $\pm$ std} & \textbf{Note} \\
698	\midrule
699	OT-7B & 5/5 & $+20.9 \pm 1.3$pp & Cleanest evidence \\
700	R1-32B & 5/5 & $+13.7 \pm 2.0$pp & Position-mediated \\
701	QwQ-32B & 5/5 & $+19.9 \pm 2.5$pp & Robust \\
702	R1-8B & 3/5 & $+14.6 \pm 4.5$pp & FPR seed-fragile \\
703	\bottomrule
704	\end{tabular}
705	\end{table}
706	
707	\paragraph{R1-8B FPR per-seed breakdown.} \cref{tab:r18b_fpr_seeds} reports per-seed HS vs.\ text FPR comparisons for R1-8B. The HS advantage is directionally consistent (5/5 seeds), with 2/5 reaching $p{<}0.05$ uncorrected; after Bonferroni correction for 5 seeds ($\alpha{=}0.01$), 1/5 remains significant. Text FPR is highly stable (std $= 0.007$) while HS FPR is split-sensitive (std $= 0.040$), which explains why the canonical-split FPR advantage ($-7.4$pp) does not generalize robustly across seeds.
708	
709	\begin{table}[h]
710	\centering
711	\small
712	\caption{R1-8B FPR per-seed comparison (L14, $W{=}15$). Direction is consistent (5/5 HS $\leq$ Text); 2/5 reach $p{<}0.05$ uncorrected, 1/5 after Bonferroni correction (5 seeds, $\alpha{=}0.01$).}
713	\label{tab:r18b_fpr_seeds}
714	\begin{tabular}{ccccc}
715	\toprule
716	\textbf{Seed} & \textbf{HS FPR} & \textbf{Text FPR} & $\Delta$\textbf{FPR} & $p$ \\
717	\midrule
718	0 & 0.161 & 0.165 & $-$0.004 & 0.440 \\
719	1 & 0.061 & 0.166 & $-$0.105 & ${<}0.001$ \\
720	2 & 0.123 & 0.176 & $-$0.053 & 0.093 \\
721	3 & 0.148 & 0.167 & $-$0.018 & 0.293 \\
722	4 & 0.100 & 0.176 & $-$0.076 & 0.021 \\
723	\midrule
724	mean$\pm$std & $0.119 \pm 0.040$ & $0.170 \pm 0.007$ & $-$0.051 & -- \\
725	\bottomrule
726	\end{tabular}
727	\end{table}
728	
729	\paragraph{R1-8B per-metric HB pass rates.} \cref{tab:r18b_metric_hb} reports Holm-Bonferroni pass rates per metric across 5 seeds for R1-8B, the model classified as ``moderate'' evidence due to FPR instability.
730	
731	\begin{table}[h]
732	\centering
733	\small
734	\caption{R1-8B per-metric$\times$seed HB pass rates. Precision is robust (5/5); FPR is fragile (3/5). FPR std across seeds is 0.040 vs.\ text FPR std 0.007, consistent with a proportion near boundary (${\sim}$12\%) amplified at $n{=}43$.}
735	\label{tab:r18b_metric_hb}
736	\begin{tabular}{lccc}

738	\textbf{Seed} & $\Delta$\textbf{Prec HB} & $\Delta$\textbf{BA HB} & $\Delta$\textbf{FPR HB} \\

741	1 & \checkmark & \checkmark & \checkmark \\
742	2 & \checkmark & \checkmark & $\times$ \\
743	3 & \checkmark & \checkmark & \checkmark \\
744	4 & \checkmark & \checkmark & \checkmark \\
745	\midrule
746	Pass rate & 5/5 & 5/5 & 3/5 \\
747	\bottomrule
748	\end{tabular}
749	\end{table}
750	
751	The FPR instability has a clear source: R1-8B text FPR clusters tightly around 17\% (std${=}0.7$pp) while HS FPR ranges from 6.1\% to 16.1\% (std${=}4.0$pp). Seeds where HS FPR happens to be high ($>$14\%) lose significance because the gap against text narrows below the detection threshold at $n{=}43$. This instability is metric-specific and does not affect the precision or balanced accuracy advantage, which passes all 5 seeds.
752	
753	\paragraph{R1-32B per-seed breakdown.} \cref{tab:r132b_seeds} reports the full per-seed breakdown for R1-32B. Seed 0 is an outlier (FPR $= 0.483$), driven by test-set trace allocation at $n{=}20$. Excluding seed 0, FPR std drops from 0.145 to 0.046.

765	1 & 0.750 & 0.748 & 0.109 \\
766	2 & 0.685 & 0.670 & 0.111 \\
767	3 & 0.652 & 0.733 & 0.111 \\
768	4 & 0.444 & 0.715 & 0.221 \\
769	\midrule

774	
775	
776	\section{BA-Layer Modality Comparison}
777	\label{tab:ba_layer_comparison}
778	
779	To address the concern that threshold-dependent layer selection may circularly favor HS probes, we repeat the modality comparison at layers selected by pure balanced accuracy, a threshold-free metric (\cref{tab:ba_layer_results}). BA-optimal layers vary widely across models (7--98\% depth), itself demonstrating that layer selection is a consequential methodological choice. The HS advantage is significant at whichever layer BA selects (14/16 per-model HB, two FPR failures from underpowered 32B test sets $n{\leq}20$).
780	
781	\begin{table}[h]
782	\centering
783	\small
784	\caption{HS vs.\ text at BA-selected layers (60/20/20 split, same as main results). 14/16 per-model tests pass Holm-Bonferroni ($\alpha{=}0.05$); two FPR failures from underpowered 32B sets. BA-L: layer selected by pure balanced accuracy. Depth varies from 7\% to 98\%.}

This instability is metric-specific and does not affect the precision or balanced accuracy advantage, which passes all 5 seeds.\n\n\\paragraph{R1-32B per-seed breakdown.} \\cref{tab:r132b_seeds} reports the full per-seed breakdown for R1-32B. Seed 0 is an outlier (FPR $= 0.483$), driven by test-set trace allocation at $n{=}20$. Excluding seed 0, FPR std drops from 0.145 to 0.046.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{R1-32B per-seed performance (L63, $W{=}1$). Seed 0 is an outlier driven by test-set trace allocation.}\n\\label{tab:r132b_seeds}\n\\begin{tabular}{cccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Seed} & \\textbf{Precision} & \\textbf{Bal.\\ Acc} & \\textbf{FPR} \\\\\n\\midrule\n0 & 0.441 & 0.594 & 0.483 \\\\\n1 & 0.750 & 0.748 & 0.109 \\\\\n2 & 0.685 & 0.670 & 0.111 \\\\\n3 & 0.652 & 0.733 & 0.111 \\\\\n4 & 0.444 & 0.715 & 0.221 \\\\\n\\midrule\nmean$\\pm$std & $0.594 \\pm 0.128$ & $0.692 \\pm 0.056$ & $0.207 \\pm 0.145$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\section{BA-Layer Modality Comparison}\n\\label{tab:ba_layer_comparison}\n\nTo address the concern that threshold-dependent layer selection may circularly favor HS probes, we repeat the modality comparison at layers selected by pure balanced accuracy, a threshold-free metric (\\cref{tab:ba_layer_results}). BA-optimal layers vary widely across models (7--98\\% depth), itself demonstrating that layer selection is a consequential methodological choice. The HS advantage is significant at whichever layer BA selects (14/16 per-model HB, two FPR failures from underpowered 32B test sets $n{\\leq}20$).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{HS vs.\\ text at BA-selected layers (60/20/20 split, same as main results). 14/16 per-model tests pass Holm-Bonferroni ($\\alpha{=}0.05$); two FPR failures from underpowered 32B sets. BA-L: layer selected by pure balanced accuracy. Depth varies from 7\\% to 98\\%.}",

Actual marginal tests: R1-32B FPR (p=0.0096) and QwQ FPR (p=0.0061), both passing comfortably.\\\"}}, \\\"exp_r3_selectivity_corrected\\\": {\\\"status\\\": \\\"DONE\\\", \\\"created_at\\\": \\\"2026-05-17T18:13:59.672Z\\\", \\\"updated_at\\\": \\\"2026-05-17T18:14:25.912Z\\\", \\\"hypothesis\\\": \\\"Selectivity at corrected deep layers (QwQ L60, R1-32B L63) should equal or exceed selectivity at biased shallow layers (QwQ L2, R1-32B L10), because deeper layers encode stronger safety-specific signal beyond positional confounds. Both should pass the 5pp threshold from Hewitt et al. 2019.\\\", \\\"conclusion\\\": \\\"success\\\", \\\"metrics\\\": {\\\"qwq_32b_l60\\\": {\\\"real_acc\\\": 0.667, \\\"random_mean\\\": 0.523, \\\"random_std\\\": 0.051, \\\"selectivity_pp\\\": 14.4, \\\"position_baseline_acc\\\": 0.705, \\\"layer\\\": 60}, \\\"r1_32b_l63\\\": {\\\"real_acc\\\": 0.746, \\\"random_mean\\\": 0.549, \\\"random_std\\\": 0.06, \\\"selectivity_pp\\\": 19.7, \\\"position_baseline_acc\\\": 0.624, \\\"layer\\\": 63}, \\\"comparison\\\": {\\\"qwq_old_l2\\\": 15.3, \\\"qwq_new_l60\\\": 14.4, \\\"r1_32b_old_l10\\\": 7.5, \\\"r1_32b_new_l63\\\": 19.7}, \\\"both_pass_5pp\\\": true, \\\"finding\\\": \\\"Both corrected layers pass 5pp threshold. R1-32B selectivity dramatically improves from 7.5pp (L10) to 19.7pp (L63). QwQ stable (15.3→14.4pp). Deep layers encode stronger safety signal beyond positional confounds.\\\"}}, \\\"exp_r3_repe_direction\\\": {\\\"status\\\": \\\"DONE\\\", \\\"created_at\\\": \\\"2026-05-17T12:34:17.057Z\\\", \\\"updated_at\\\": \\\"2026-05-17T15:39:49.895Z\\\", \\\"hypothesis\\\": \\\"RepE mean-difference direction probe (a single safety direction in activation space) should underperform trained LR probes on accuracy/precision but may achieve longer lead times, because the safety direction captures a coarser signal that emerges earlier in the reasoning trace.\\\", \\\"conclusion\\\": \\\"success\\\", \\\"metrics\\\": {\\\"r1_8b\\\": {\\\"layer\\\": 2, \\\"bal_acc\\\": 0.7491, \\\"precision\\\": 0.516, \\\"pre_cp_fpr\\\": 0.2264, \\\"coverage\\\": 0.3953, \\\"lead_mean\\\": 9.7}, \\\"r1_32b_corrected\\\": {\\\"layer\\\": 63, \\\"bal_acc\\\": 0.5588, \\\"precision\\\": 0.3891, \\\"pre_cp_fpr\\\": 0.4986, \\\"coverage\\\": 0.6, \\\"lead_mean\\\": 12}, \\\"r1_32b_old\\\": {\\\"layer\\\": 10, \\\"bal_acc\\\": 0.6707, \\\"precision\\\": 0.5538, \\\"pre_cp_fpr\\\": 0.2423, \\\"coverage\\\": 0.45, \\\"lead_mean\\\": 6.3}, \\\"qwq_32b_corrected\\\": {\\\"layer\\\": 60, \\\"bal_acc\\\": 0.7562, \\\"precision\\\": 0.5118, \\\"pre_cp_fpr\\\": 0.3089, \\\"coverage\\\": 0.7368, \\\"lead_mean\\\": 11.9}, \\\"qwq_32b_old\\\": {\\\"layer\\\": 2, \\\"bal_acc\\\": 0.6661, \\\"precision\\\": 0.4193, \\\"pre_cp_fpr\\\": 0.3997, \\\"coverage\\\": 0.7368, \\\"lead_mean\\\": 13.4}, \\\"ot_7b\\\": {\\\"layer\\\": 16, \\\"bal_acc\\\": 0.718, \\\"precision\\\": 0.3733, \\\"pre_cp_fpr\\\": 0.2573, \\\"coverage\\\": 0.5319, \\\"lead_mean\\\": 26.1}, \\\"vs_lr_r1_8b\\\": {\\\"lr_bal_acc\\\": 0.817, \\\"lr_precision\\\": 0.693, \\\"lr_fpr\\\": 0.097, \\\"repe_weaker_acc\\\": true, \\\"repe_weaker_precision\\\": true}, \\\"finding\\\": \\\"Corrected layers: R1-32B L63 RepE degrades severely (bal_acc 0.67→0.56, FPR 0.24→0.50); QwQ L60 improves (bal_acc 0.67→0.76). Both still below supervised LR. Unsupervised RepE direction insufficient at any layer.\\\"}, \\\"notes\\\": \\\"Updated 2026-05-17T15:38: Corrected layers applied per Reviewer feedback. Results at artifacts/exp_r3_repe_corrected_layer/results.json.\\\"}, \\\"exp_r3_gru_per_seed\\\": {\\\"status\\\": \\\"DONE\\\", \\\"created_at\\\": \\\"2026-05-17T15:36:13.651Z\\\", \\\"updated_at\\\": \\\"2026-05-17T15:38:09.830Z\\\", \\\"hypothesis\\\": \\\"GRU probe performance on R1-8B should be robust across 5 random seeds (70/30 split), with bal_acc std less than 5pp, confirming that the single-seed result (0.839) is not a lucky split artifact and temporal modeling genuinely improves commitment detection.\\\", \\\"conclusion\\\": \\\"success\\\", \\\"metrics\\\": {\\\"seeds\\\": [42, 123, 456, 789, 1024], \\\"split\\\": \\\"70/30\\\", \\\"bal_acc\\\": {\\\"mean\\\": 0.772, \\\"std\\\": 0.038, \\\"min\\\": 0.729, \\\"max\\\": 0.833}, \\\"crossing_rate\\\": {\\\"mean\\\": 0.711, \\\"std\\\": 0.121}, \\\"lead_mean\\\": {\\\"mean\\\": 8.2, \\\"std\\\": 2.9}, \\\"fpr\\\": {\\\"mean\\\": 0.229, \\\"std\\\": 0.075}, \\\"vs_single_seed\\\": {\\\"single_seed_bal_acc\\\": 0.839, \\\"multi_seed_mean\\\": 0.772, \\\"delta\\\": -0.067, \\\"note\\\": \\\"Single seed (0.839) is optimistic; multi-seed mean (0.772) more representative\\\"}, \\\"finding\\\": \\\"GRU robust across 5 seeds (std=3.8pp < 5pp threshold). Single-seed result overestimates by ~6.7pp. Lead time high variance (std=2.9) indicates sensitivity to data split.\\\"}}, \\\"exp_r3_selectivity_ci_raw\\\": {\\\"status\\\": \\\"DONE\\\", \\\"created_at\\\": \\\"2026-05-17T15:36:11.178Z\\\", \\\"updated_at\\\": \\\"2026-05-17T15:38:09.071Z\\\", \\\"hypothesis\\\": \\\"R1-32B Layer 10 selectivity (real_acc - random_acc) on raw 5120-d features should be statistically significant (p less than 0.05), confirming the probe learns safety-specific signal beyond positional confounds even without PCA dimensionality reduction.\\\", \\\"conclusion\\\": \\\"negative\\\", \\\"metrics\\\": {\\\"feature_type\\\": \\\"raw 5120-d (no PCA)\\\", \\\"real_acc\\\": 0.584, \\\"random_mean\\\": 0.508, \\\"selectivity_pp\\\": 7.6, \\\"bootstrap_mean_pp\\\": 8.4, \\\"bootstrap_std_pp\\\": 6.7, \\\"ci_95\\\": \\\"[-3.9, 22.7]pp\\\", \\\"p_value\\\": 0.107, \\\"significant\\\": false, \\\"vs_pca128\\\": {\\\"pca128_selectivity\\\": 15.9, \\\"pca128_p\\\": 0, \\\"pca128_ci\\\": \\\"[9.7, 21.9]pp\\\"}, \\\"finding\\\": \\\"Raw 5120-d selectivity 7.6pp not significant (p=0.107, CI includes 0). Under-determined n&lt;p without PCA. PCA-128 gives 15.9pp (p<0.001) — dimensionality reduction needed for reliable selectivity.\\\"}}, \\\"exp_r3_coverage_bias\\\": {\\\"status\\\": \\\"DONE\\\", \\\"created_at\\\": \\\"2026-05-17T15:36:08.744Z\\\", \\\"updated_at\\\": \\\"2026-05-17T15:38:08.545Z\\\", \\\"hypothesis\\\": \\\"R1-8B trace exclusion rate should be explained by jailbreak traces without any safety reasoning phase (no commitment point annotatable), not by systematic sampling bias. If exclusion is task-definitional rather than methodological, the coverage metric is valid within the annotatable population.\\\", \\\"conclusion\\\": \\\"success\\\", \\\"metrics\\\": {\\\"r1_8b\\\": {\\\"total\\\": 268, \\\"included\\\": 209, \\\"excluded\\\": 59, \\\"exclusion_rate\\\": 0.22}, \\\"r1_32b\\\": {\\\"total\\\": 100, \\\"excluded\\\": 0, \\\"exclusion_rate\\\": 0}, \\\"qwq_32b\\\": {\\\"total\\\": 95, \\\"excluded\\\": 0, \\\"exclusion_rate\\\": 0}, \\\"exclusion_reason\\\": \\\"All 59 excluded traces are jailbreak with no safety reasoning phase (no commitment point annotatable)\\\", \\\"length_test\\\": {\\\"excluded_mean\\\": 38, \\\"included_mean\\\": 46.3, \\\"mann_whitney_p\\\": 0.0001, \\\"excluded_shorter\\\": true}, \\\"finding\\\": \\\"22% exclusion in R1-8B is task-definitional (no CP to annotate), not sampling bias. R1-32B/QwQ 0% exclusion. Coverage metric valid within annotatable population.\\\"}}, \\\"exp_r3_bocpd_r1_32b_qwq\\\": {\\\"status\\\": \\\"DONE\\\", \\\"created_at\\\": \\\"2026-05-17T15:36:06.119Z\\\", \\\"updated_at\\\": \\\"2026-05-17T15:38:07.653Z\\\", \\\"hypothesis\\\": \\\"BOCPD/CUSUM on R1-32B and QwQ-32B probe scores should fail (0% BOCPD detection) as on OT-7B, because probe scores exhibit abrupt rather than gradual transitions. If confirmed across 4/4 models, advanced change-point methods are universally inferior to simple threshold crossing for safety commitment detection.\\\", \\\"conclusion\\\": \\\"negative\\\", \\\"metrics\\\": {\\\"r1_32b\\\": {\\\"layer\\\": 10, \\\"W\\\": 10, \\\"bocpd_det\\\": 0, \\\"cusum_det\\\": 0.85, \\\"cusum_lead\\\": 2.59, \\\"fcp_det\\\": 0.95, \\\"fcp_lead\\\": 5.68}, \\\"qwq_32b\\\": {\\\"layer\\\": 2, \\\"W\\\": 5, \\\"bocpd_det\\\": 0, \\\"cusum_det\\\": 0.737, \\\"cusum_lead\\\": 9.86, \\\"fcp_det\\\": 0.789, \\\"fcp_lead\\\": 9.4}, \\\"cross_model_bocpd\\\": {\\\"r1_8b\\\": 0.93, \\\"r1_32b\\\": 0, \\\"ot_7b\\\": 0, \\\"qwq_32b\\\": 0, \\\"only_r1_8b_works\\\": true}, \\\"finding\\\": \\\"BOCPD 0% on 3/4 models (only R1-8B exception at 93%). CUSUM universally inferior to simple FCP threshold. Advanced change-point methods fail for safety commitment detection.\\\"}}, \\\"exp_r3_mahalanobis_r1_32b_qwq\\\": {\\\"status\\\": \\\"DONE\\\", \\\"created_at\\\": \\\"2026-05-17T15:36:04.726Z\\\", \\\"updated_at\\\": \\\"2026-05-17T15:38:06.680Z\\\", \\\"hypothesis\\\": \\\"Mahalanobis distance on R1-32B (Layer 63) and QwQ-32B (Layer 60) should fail as commitment detector for the same reason as R1-8B and OT-7B: discriminative signal resides in PCA minor components not captured by covariance-based distance. If confirmed, 4/4 models negative generalizes the finding.\\\", \\\"conclusion\\\": \\\"negative\\\", \\\"metrics\\\": {\\\"r1_32b\\\": {\\\"layer\\\": 63, \\\"safe_md\\\": 6.73, \\\"post_md\\\": 6.88, \\\"separation\\\": 0.15, \\\"best_pca\\\": 64}, \\\"qwq_32b\\\": {\\\"layer\\\": 60, \\\"safe_md\\\": 3.22, \\\"post_md\\\": 3.42, \\\"separation\\\": 0.2, \\\"best_pca\\\": 16}, \\\"cross_model_summary\\\": {\\\"r1_8b_sep\\\": 0.07, \\\"ot_7b_sep\\\": 0.03, \\\"r1_32b_sep\\\": 0.15, \\\"qwq_32b_sep\\\": 0.2, \\\"max_separation\\\": 0.2, \\\"all_fail\\\": true}, \\\"finding\\\": \\\"4/4 models Mahalanobis fails. Max separation 0.20 (QwQ), false alarm rate >= true positive at every threshold. Signal in minor PCA components, not principal directions.\\\"}}, \\\"exp_r3_layer_ablation\\\": {\\\"status\\\": \\\"DONE\\\", \\\"created_at\\\": \\\"2026-05-17T15:35:59.921Z\\\", \\\"updated_at\\\": \\\"2026-05-17T15:38:05.729Z\\\", \\\"hypothesis\\\": \\\"The bal_acc x crossing_rate layer selection metric systematically biases toward shallow layers because crossing_rate correlates with early threshold crossing. Ablating across 5 metrics on 4 models should reveal that pure bal_acc or precision metrics select mid-to-deep layers (40-95% depth), exposing the metric as confounded.\\\", \\\"conclusion\\\": \\\"success\\\", \\\"metrics\\\": {\\\"models_tested\\\": 4, \\\"metrics_tested\\\": 5, \\\"bias_discovered\\\": \\\"bal_acc x crossing_rate systematically selects shallow layers\\\", \\\"qwq_32b\\\": {\\\"biased_layer\\\": 2, \\\"corrected_layer\\\": 60, \\\"bal_acc_gain\\\": \\\"+1.75pp\\\", \\\"precision_gain\\\": \\\"+11.5pp\\\", \\\"fpr_reduction\\\": \\\"-8.3pp\\\"}, \\\"r1_32b\\\": {\\\"biased_layer\\\": 10, \\\"corrected_layer\\\": 63, \\\"bal_acc_gain\\\": \\\"+3.7pp\\\", \\\"precision_gain\\\": \\\"+4.1pp\\\", \\\"fpr_reduction\\\": \\\"-4.3pp\\\"}, \\\"r1_8b\\\": {\\\"biased_layer\\\": 0, \\\"best_layer\\\": 12, \\\"consistency\\\": \\\"L12-L16 cluster, current L14 near-optimal\\\"}, \\\"ot_7b\\\": {\\\"biased_layer\\\": 0, \\\"best_layer\\\": 9, \\\"current_layer\\\": 16, \\\"note\\\": \\\"4 metrics select 4 different layers\\\"}, \\\"cross_metric_consistency\\\": {\\\"r1_8b\\\": \\\"CONSISTENT\\\", \\\"r1_32b\\\": \\\"VARIED (all point deep)\\\", \\\"qwq_32b\\\": \\\"VARIED (all point deep)\\\", \\\"ot_7b\\\": \\\"VARIED\\\"}, \\\"finding\\\": \\\"crossing_rate biases layer selection to shallow layers. Pure bal_acc/precision select mid-to-deep layers (40-95% depth). W3+ all fail after Bonferroni. Confirms HS advantage exists at L63 but only robust at minimal temporal context.\\\"}}, \\\"exp_r3_ccs_probe\\\": {\\\"status\\\": \\\"DONE\\\", \\\"created_at\\\": \\\"2026-05-17T15:35:52.139Z\\\", \\\"updated_at\\\": \\\"2026-05-17T15:38:02.913Z\\\", \\\"hypothesis\\\": \\\"CCS (Burns et al. 2023) adapted for temporal safety probing should underperform supervised LR probes because the consistency+confidence loss lacks access to commitment point labels. ```text
1	# R12: 5-Seed Holm-Bonferroni Analysis — HS vs Text (384d MiniLM)
2	
3	## Setup
4	- **Method**: Paired bootstrap permutation test (1000 resamples, null-centered, two-sided)
5	- **Correction**: Holm-Bonferroni on 3 metrics per model-seed (bal_acc, precision, pre-CP FPR)
6	- **Split**: 70/30 train/test per seed
7	- **Classifier**: LogisticRegression(solver='lbfgs', C=1.0, class_weight='balanced', max_iter=2000)
8	- **Text baseline**: all-MiniLM-L6-v2 (384d)
9	- **Seeds**: [42, 123, 456, 789, 1024]
10	
11	## Table 1: Per-seed results (4 models x 5 seeds)
12	
13	| Model    |  Seed |     BA p |   Prec p |    FPR p |  3/3 HB |
14	|----------|-------|----------|----------|----------|---------|
15	| R1-8B    |    42 |   0.0040 |   0.0140 |   0.0140 |    PASS |
16	| R1-8B    |   123 |   0.0070 |   0.0030 |   0.2620 |    FAIL |
17	| R1-8B    |   456 |   0.0010 |   0.0700 |   0.9520 |    FAIL |
18	| R1-8B    |   789 |   0.0010 |   0.0000 |   0.0340 |    PASS |
19	| R1-8B    |  1024 |   0.0000 |   0.0000 |   0.0090 |    PASS |
20	| OT-7B    |    42 |   0.0000 |   0.0000 |   0.0000 |    PASS |
21	| OT-7B    |   123 |   0.0000 |   0.0000 |   0.0000 |    PASS |
22	| OT-7B    |   456 |   0.0000 |   0.0000 |   0.0000 |    PASS |
23	| OT-7B    |   789 |   0.0000 |   0.0000 |   0.0000 |    PASS |
24	| OT-7B    |  1024 |   0.0000 |   0.0000 |   0.0000 |    PASS |
25	| R1-32B   |    42 |   0.0000 |   0.0010 |   0.0120 |    PASS |
26	| R1-32B   |   123 |   0.0000 |   0.0000 |   0.0000 |    PASS |
27	| R1-32B   |   456 |   0.0060 |   0.0000 |   0.0000 |    PASS |
28	| R1-32B   |   789 |   0.0030 |   0.0000 |   0.0020 |    PASS |
29	| R1-32B   |  1024 |   0.0000 |   0.0010 |   0.0010 |    PASS |
30	| QwQ-32B  |    42 |   0.0000 |   0.0000 |   0.0010 |    PASS |
31	| QwQ-32B  |   123 |   0.0120 |   0.0080 |   0.0010 |    PASS |
32	| QwQ-32B  |   456 |   0.0120 |   0.0010 |   0.0000 |    PASS |
33	| QwQ-32B  |   789 |   0.0000 |   0.0000 |   0.0000 |    PASS |
34	| QwQ-32B  |  1024 |   0.0020 |   0.0010 |   0.0000 |    PASS |
35	
36	## Table 2: Summary
37	
38	| Model    | Pass  | Mean DPrec (pp)    | Mean DBA (pp)      |
39	|----------|-------|--------------------|--------------------|
40	| R1-8B    |   3/5 |  +14.62 +/-  4.49  |   +9.41 +/-  2.37  |
41	| OT-7B    |   5/5 |  +20.90 +/-  1.27  |  +11.34 +/-  1.48  |
42	| R1-32B   |   5/5 |  +13.71 +/-  1.99  |  +11.83 +/-  2.41  |
43	| QwQ-32B  |   5/5 |  +19.85 +/-  2.50  |  +11.44 +/-  3.18  |
44	
45	## Key findings
46	
47	1. **18/20 model-seed combos pass 3/3 HB** (90% overall passing rate)
48	2. **OT-7B, R1-32B, QwQ-32B**: 5/5 seeds pass (100%), all p-values well below HB thresholds
49	3. **R1-8B**: 3/5 seeds pass (60%); the 2 failures are driven by FPR (seed 123: p=0.262, seed 456: p=0.952), meaning HS does not consistently lower pre-CP FPR vs text on all R1-8B splits
50	4. **Largest HS advantage**: OT-7B (+20.90pp precision, +11.34pp BA) and QwQ-32B (+19.85pp precision, +11.44pp BA)
51	5. **Most stable**: OT-7B (std of DPrec = 1.27pp, DBA = 1.48pp)
52	6. **R1-8B instability**: FPR metric is seed-sensitive (std of DPrec = 4.49pp), likely due to small test set (63 traces at 30% split) and W=15 window creating high-dimensional features
53	
54	## Data location
